91  Here,  a  form  of  action  explanation  is  assumed  that  answers  the  why  question  (in  the  sense  of  "Why  did  you  do  that?")  by  specifying  the  intention  or  purpose  of  the  action  (intentional,  
teleological  explanation  of  action,  "intentionalism").  It  is  opposed  to  a  form  of  action  explanation  that  provides  for  the  statement  of  the  causes  triggering  the  action  as  an  answer  (causal  action  
explanation,  "causalism").  Whether  an  action  should  be  explained  intentionally  or  causally  is  not  a  question  of  the  truth/falseness  of  the  explanation,  but  a  question  of  the  contextual  adequacy  of  
the  interpretation  of  the  action.  For  the  norm  sciences,  the  question  of  the  intention  or  the  purpose  of  the  action  is  in  the  foreground  without  denying  the  possibility  of  a  causal  explanation  of  the  
action.  See  Horn,  C.;  Lohrer,  G.  (ed.)  (2010):  Reasons  and  purposes.  Texts  on  current  action  theory.  Berlin.

3.4.1  Basic  philosophical  determination  of  being  human
This  triad  of  reason,  freedom  and  responsibility  characterizes  today  both  the  everyday  life
Morality  as  well  as  the  legal  system  to  a  large  extent.  The  focus  is  on  the  phenomenon
Action,  reason  and  responsibility  are  at  the  center  of  humanistic112  philosophy.
Artificial  intelligence,  should  have  a  place  and  are  an  expression  of  perceived  Ver
The  different  ways  in  which  the  concept  of  humanism  is  used  in  philosophy  are  correct  in  some  respects
deals,  especially  with  human/technology  or  human/machine  constellations,  a  large  number  of  other  
perspectives  are  also  pursued  (Heßler,  M.;  Liggieri,  K.  (2020):  Technikanthropologie.  Handbuch  fur  
Wissenschaft  und  Studium.  Baden-Baden).  These  include,  for  example,  homo  faber  and  homo  creator,  
trans-  and  post-humanist  positions  and  the  actor-network  theory.  The  close  connection  of  ethical  questions  to  
the  concepts  of  freedom  and  responsibility  implies  that  these  are  not  specifically  considered  in  the  considerations,  
but  that  the  humanistic  perspective  is  placed  in  the  middle.

7.3.2  Filter  bubbles  and  echo  chambers
appear281  and  have  a  particularly  memorable  effect282,  even  if  they  are  later  discredited  or  considered
Filter  bubbles  and  echo  chambers  are  metaphors  based  on  the  hypothesis  that  the  strong
Algorithmic  curating's  focus  on  individual  preferences  can  result  in  the
controversially  marked.  This  applies  all  the  more  if  content  is  distributed  within  a  personal  network  whose

enforce.  Therefore,  there  must  be  a  clear  and  recognizable  assignment  of  responsibility
Barocas  and  Selbst  (2016)  provide  an  extremely  helpful  overview  of  possible  sources  of  bias  and  
discrimination  in  data-based  systems.  In  addition  to  the  selection  of  target  variables,  labels  or  relevant  
characteristics,  the  data  used  is  of  crucial  importance  here.  In  this  case,  problems  can  arise  not  only  due  to  
incorrectness  of  the  data  itself,  but  also  due  to  over-  and  under-representation,  ie  if,  for  example,  people  are  over-  
or  under-represented  in  data  sets.  There  is  also  the  problem  of  so-called  proxy  variables:  sensitive  categories  
such  as  gender,  religious  affiliation  or  sexual  orientation  can  sometimes  be  derived  from  other  data  points  such  
as  hobbies,  shopping  behavior,  place  of  residence,  etc.  Due  to  this  so-called  redundant  encoding,  people  can  be  
systematically  discriminated  against  by  software  because  of  their  gender  or  their  sexual  or  religious  orientation,  
even  if  this  information  was  not  collected  at  all:  precisely  because  these  categories  can  be  derived  from  other  
data.  Most  of  the  sources  of  bias  and  discrimination  described  above  are  not  intentional,  but  the  result  of  
methodological  decisions  or
227   
Mistake.  In  principle,  however,  it  is  also  possible  to  intentionally  incorporate  discriminatory  effects  into  
software  systems,  a  problem  which  Barocas  and  Selbst  call  masking.  See  Barocas,  S.;  Selbst,  D.  (2016):  
Big  Data's  Disparate  Impact.  In:  California  Law  Review  104  (3),  671-732.
Friedman,  B.;  Nissenbaum,  H.  (1996):  Bias  in  Computer  Systems.  In:  ACM  Transactions  on  Information   
Systems,  14  (3),  330-347  (DOI:  10.1145/230538.230561).

400  Technologies  that  are  used  in  the  context  of  the  decision  to  suspend  the  sentence  on  probation  (e.g.  the  COMPAS  
software)  can  also  be  classified  as  predictive  policing  procedures  insofar  as  they  are  also  based  on  the  individual  dangerousness  
of  the  offender.  If  the  latter  is  given  a  negative  social  prognosis,  this  precludes  a  suspended  sentence  on  probation.  This  means  
that  the  probation  decision  is  essentially  one  based  on  the  law  on  averting  danger.  In  German  law,  however,  it  must  be  taken  
into  account  that  the  work  of  courts  (which  decide  on  the  suspension  of  sentences  on  probation)  is  traditionally  separated  from  
police  work.  It  is  therefore  less  common  to  classify  the  probation  decision  as  a  police  activity,  which  at  least  formally  speaks  
against  the  classification  as  predictive  policing.
